- **display 58.1**, p. 58, proof of Lemma 5.4: different. The paper writes the Faà di Bruno term structure of a mixed space-time derivative of χ(aq) with q = q(z,t): a prefactor a^k χ^{(k)}(aq) times a product of k derivatives of q whose multi-indices sum to (a',b'). No Lean declaration forms that expansion. What the repository states instead is (i) the exact one-variable chain rule `scaledCutoff_iteratedDeriv`, iteratedDeriv n (scaledCutoff a) q = a^n · χ^{(n)}(a q) — this is the paper's term in the degenerate case where q is itself the variable, so every ∂q factor is 1 and only k = n survives; (ii) the k = 1 case for a genuine composite χ(q(w.1,·)), as the `have hc` step at GenericSolenoidalRealization.lean:81, giving ∇(χ∘q) = χ'(q)·∇q; and (iii) for general order, the norm-level composition bound `composition_jet_bound` (a wrapper of Mathlib's norm_iteratedFDerivWithin_comp_le), ‖D^m(g∘f)‖ ≤ m!·C·D^m, which is what `cutoff_jet_bound` actually uses to reach the same conclusion without ever naming the individual chain-rule terms. There is also no z/t split: the Lean works with one total Fréchet order throughout. The surrounding result (the derivative bound of the next display) is proved, so the missing expansion costs nothing. - **display 58.2**, p. 58, proof of Lemma 5.4: different. Both statements bound derivatives of the composed cutoff by a negative power of q with a constant independent of the scale a, and the quantifier order matches (the constant is chosen before a, as the paper's 'with constants independent of a' requires). What differs is the exponent bookkeeping. The paper separates the orders: a' Cartesian space derivatives cost q^{-D} each and b' time derivatives cost q^{-1} each, with 0 < D < 1 coming from its hypothesis (5.28), \|∂_z^a ∂_t^b q\| ≤ C_{a,b} q^{1-aD-b}. The Lean has no D and no space/time split: `cutoff_jet_bound` takes a single total Fréchet order m, assumes ‖D^k q‖ ≤ B_k q^{1-k} (the D = 1 case of (5.28)), and concludes ‖D^m(χ∘(a·q))‖ ≤ C q^{-m}. Setting D = 1 and m = a' + b' makes the two coincide; for D < 1 the paper's bound is strictly sharper on q ≤ 1, since q^{-a'D-b'} ≤ q^{-a'-b'}. The Lean's hypothesis is correspondingly weaker (the paper's (5.28) implies it, because 1 - aD - b ≥ 1 - a - b and q ≤ 1), so the two implications are a consistent D = 1 specialization rather than a contradiction. The sharpening is a weaker intermediate bound that still suffices: the extra loss is absorbed into the per-order exponent reduction, the paper's ℓ'_m and the Lean's `CutStageEstimates.cutLoss`, and the downstream stage bound (`cut_product_bound`, `exists_cut_stage_constants`) is proved with it. Norms differ cosmetically too: the paper's \|·\|_m-style absolute value of a single mixed partial against the Lean's operator norm of the full iterated Fréchet derivative. Lean: `NavierStokes.CutStageEstimates.cutoff_jet_bound` (states, NavierStokes/CutStageEstimates.lean:95); `NavierStokes.PhysicalCoordinateBounds.q_derivative_bound` (uses, NavierStokes/PhysicalCoordinateBounds.lean:410).

No multi-index partial-derivative notation ∂^I was found anywhere in the NavierStokes namespace: the Lean measures regularity with the total Fréchet jet ‖iteratedFDeriv ℝ j (f n) x‖ and quantifies over the order j ≤ m, not over a five-component multi-index. The chart's five real coordinates do exist (LocalSignedRequest.Point = ℝ × ((ℝ×ℝ) × (ℝ×ℝ)), with the slow pair (Z,T) and harmonic pair (H₁,H₂) read off by BaseContextAssembly.slowCoordinates), so the paper's I ∈ ℕ₀⁵ has a referent, but MemClass itself is stated for an abstract normed space D and never names R, Z, T, H₁, H₂. The two encodings are interchangeable in the direction the paper uses — ‖∂^I a‖ ≤ ‖iteratedFDeriv ℝ \|I\| a‖ for every multi-index I of length \|I\|, since each coordinate partial is an evaluation of the \|I\|-linear iterated derivative on unit basis vectors — so the Lean bound is at least as strong as the paper's per-multi-index bound at the same order. I did not find a Lean lemma stating that comparison, and the converse direction (reconstituting the iterated-Fréchet norm from the coordinate partials) costs a dimensional constant; since this display only fixes notation for the bounds (6.24), (6.25) and (6.29), which the Lean states in its own notation, the surrounding results are still proved. Recorded as packaging/notation, not a mathematical gap. Rated low because the cross-label cancellation the paper draws from this line is what the Lean proves, by the germ route. - **display 79.2**, p. 79, proof of Proposition 7.2: lean weaker. The display is the degree recursion of the Step 2 induction: with source jets of S_*-degree b_I and coefficient jets of degree c_J (order-dependent), the I-th solution jet has degree at most b'_I = 1 + max{b_I, max_{0<J≤I}(c_J + b'_{I-J})}. The Lean states only the case where one exponent m bounds every input jet of order ≤ N (K·S^m for the coefficient, w·K·S^m·W for the source) and concludes with exponent (m+1)(n+1) at order n. On that case the two agree exactly: b_I = c_J = m gives b'_0 = 1+m and b'_I = 1 + m + b'_{I-1} = (m+1)(I+1), so the Lean statement follows from the display by specialisation. The converse fails when the degrees vary with the order. Taking m to be the maximum of the input degrees (the first run's reduction) gives a larger exponent than the paper's, and since S ≥ 1 that is a weaker bound, so it does not give the display's "at most". Example I checked by hand: b ≡ 0, c_1 = 0, c_2 = 10 gives b'_2 = 12 from the display; norm_jet_solution_le_polynomial with m = 10, N = 2 gives 33, and the unresolved product form norm_jet_solution_le with N = 2, M = K·S^10 gives degree 1 + 2·11 = 23. Recovering the order-by-order recursion would need a Leibniz bound that tracks each order separately, and the only cross-term bound stated, norm_crossJet_le, takes one constant M for all coefficient orders and one B for all lower solution orders. So the order-dependent case of the display is not stated and does not follow by instantiating stated declarations. The surrounding result is unaffected: (7.14)-(7.15) assert only that some finite degrees b'_I exist ("Constants and degrees may depend on M, I, and the prescribed source bounds", p. 78), and the later mentions I found in the text layer (pp. 79-80 region, "changes constants and polynomial degrees") likewise need only finiteness, which the Lean exponent supplies. I did not trace every consumer of the Lean jet bound; the one I opened (ParticularWaveBounds.lean:139) supplies a single uniform exponent m+1. Lean: `NavierStokes.WeightedODEJets.norm_jet_solution_le` (states, NavierStokes/WeightedODEJets.lean:477); `NavierStokes.WeightedODEJets.amplitude_le_polynomial` (states, NavierStokes/WeightedODEJets.lean:562); `NavierStokes.WeightedODEJets.norm_jet_solution_le_polynomial` (states, NavierStokes/WeightedODEJets.lean:621); `NavierStokes.WeightedODEJets.norm_crossJet_le` (uses, NavierStokes/WeightedODEJets.lean:299); `NavierStokes.CommonCoverSolve.LinearData.anchoredSolve_jets_le_polynomial` (states, NavierStokes/CommonCoverSolve.lean:892). - **display 81.1**, p. 81, proof of Lemma 7.4: different. No declaration forms the quotient r = z₋/z₊ or asserts that it is differentiable with this derivative; I searched the sources and the index and found no Riccati/quotient ODE anywhere (the only occurrences of this polynomial in NavierStokes/ are GrowingMode.lean lines 76 and 92). The Lean takes another route, which its own module docstring states: "The invariant region is proved using a quadratic boundary function, without dividing by the growing coordinate or assuming its positivity along the solution." In place of r it uses barrier t = (z t 1)² - r²(z t 0)² with r a *constant* half-width (a name collision with the paper's ratio function), and shows barrier ≤ 0 by a boundary-derivative argument. The two formulas are linked exactly: writing u = z₋/z₊, d/dt[z₋² - r²z₊²] = 2 z₊² u u' + 2 z₊ z₊'(u² - r²), so at u = ±r the barrier derivative is ±2 r z₊² times the display's right-hand side — and that right-hand side appears verbatim as the subject of upper_edge_factor_negative. The initial condition r(0) = 0 is stated, in the modal coordinates, by primary_initial (z₋(a) = 0 with z₊(a) = P(a) > 0). Only the paper's intermediate formula, not its conclusion, is missing: the cone bound it is used to prove is established (positive_invariant_cone, scaled_positive_invariant_cone). The viscous term of (7.22) does appear as a formula, in `viscous_coefficient_nonneg`, which writes the paper's d = εk^2\|n_Φ\|^2 with the harmonic factor j^2 of (7.5), but only its sign is asserted there; the corresponding statement about the energy is the inequality `damping_dissipative`, not the equality -εk^2\|n_Φ\|^2\|t\|^2. The Lean packages it as ZeroOrderBounds with R = sqrt(S_n). Where the Lean hypotheses hold, all three clauses follow by formal steps: - \|det H\| >= c/S_* is the `determinant` field after normalizedMatrix_det. - \|\|H^{-1}\|\| <= C sqrt(S_*) and y_sigma <= C sqrt(S_*)\|T\| are stated by no declaration. Both follow by 2x2 adjugate/Cramer algebra from the `entries` and `determinant` fields, since `weights` is by definition cramerNumerator / det. - c sqrt(S_*)\|T\| <= y_sigma is exists_prepared_bounds at zeta = targetAmplitude v p, rewritten with actualTarget_eq_direction_all. targetAmplitude is the norm of actualTarget by definition. Reaching the face needs a limit or continuity of H and T up to tau = 0, which is not a formal step. 2. **Points of the enlarged box with q/Q outside [1/2, 2] and X_a < X < X_b**, for the y clause only. The cone-margin inputs (Prepared.target_margin, target_unit, target_actual, prepared_model_mem, actualTarget_eq_direction_all) are all restricted to referenceSet. Extending the margin needs the continuity argument the paper invokes on p. 74, which the repository does not state there. Clauses 1 and 2 are not affected by gap 2: exists_family_bounds quantifies over every p in the carrier and can be instantiated with the target direction at the representative. The Lean also states things the display does not: the entry bound \|H_ij\| <= C/sqrt(S_*), which is the content of (7.28), and the lower bound in terms of the moving weight. Because part of the display's range neither is stated nor follows formally, the class is lean_weaker. Not verified: that the paper's unspecified "one fixed factor" enlargement equals the Lean's two-mesh cell, that the Lean covariance matrix is the paper's H, and whether the tau = 0 face is recovered elsewhere in the repository by a separate limiting argument. - **display 83.4**, p. 83, proof of Proposition 7.5: different. The explicit Faa di Bruno expansion of the display — the sum over compositions I_1+...+I_n = I with coefficients c_{I_1,...,I_n} and the factor y_sigma^{1/2-n} — occurs nowhere in the Lean; no declaration forms it, and the repository contains no Faa di Bruno / OrderedFinpartition machinery at all. The Lean reaches the same destination (the derivative bound of the next display) by two norm-level routes instead. Route one: `comp_jet_bound` wraps Mathlib's norm_iteratedFDerivWithin_comp_le, which is the quantitative shadow of the same combinatorics, and feeds it the one-dimensional outer-derivative identity `iteratedDeriv_rpow` (d^n/dt^n t^p = rpowCoeff p n * t^{p-n}); at p = 1/2 that supplies exactly the paper's y^{1/2-n} factor, but only inside a norm estimate, with the composition combinatorics collapsed into the factor n.factorial. Route two: `sqrt_class` avoids the composition entirely, writing sqrt(g) = 2 * (g / (2 sqrt g)) and invoking a signed-quotient class lemma. The paper uses the display only to derive the next bound, which both routes prove, so the surrounding result is not affected. (i) General-e counterparts, all with M = 0, v = 0 (no auxiliary shift): IntegratedMeanBalances.radialDivergence c f r = deriv f r + c/r·f r is 𝒟_e for an arbitrary index; SignedStressPrimitive.primitive P e F = TransportPrimitive.compactIntegral (cutoff P) 0 0 (weightedSource e F) is 𝓘(R^e f) − χ𝓙(R^e f) verbatim, with χ = cutoff P a smooth 0→1 cutoff with plateaus strictly inside (a,b) (cutoff_zero :78, cutoff_one :82) whose derivative is the density (cutoff_deriv :98); sigma P e F = −inversePower P e R · primitive P e F = −R^{-e}𝓘_c(R^e f), i.e. −T_e f (its integral form −(∫_0^R r^e·adjusted)/R^e is sigma_eq_negative_primitive :272, and primitive_eq_integral :244 shows the moment-density form and the 𝓘 − χ𝓙 form are the same expression rewritten, not a different construction); bumpCorrection P e F z = momentDensity P e R · mass e F = R^{-e}(∂_Rχ)𝓙(R^e f) is 𝒜_e verbatim (total_weightedSource :226 identifies mass e F with 𝓙(R^e f)). angular_divergence :352 states 𝒟_2(sigma P 2 F) = −adjusted P 2 F, which under sigma = −T_2 and adjusted = f − 𝒜_2 f is the e = 2 case of (8.7); axial_divergence :366 is the e = 1 case. (ii) What is not there: the shifted operators, those whose integrand carries the transport shift y + M((R')^{d_r} − R^{d_r})v_r of (8.4)/(8.5), are written out only at e = 0 (physicalCompact/compactPrimitive, physicalAlias, with physicalAlias_eq_cutoff_derivative giving 𝒜_0 = (∂_Rχ_m)𝓙) and at e = 1 (streamPotential = R⁻¹𝓘_c(Rf), streamGamma = D_r + 1/R, and streamGamma_eq_desired_sub_alias, which is (8.7) at e = 1). No declaration was found that instantiates the shifted operator at e = 2, and no declaration was found defining the shifted T_e, 𝒜_e, 𝒟_e as one e-indexed family. That is the whole of the gap: a definition the paper writes once for e ∈ {0,1,2} is, in its shifted form, written twice. - **(8.7)**, p. 90, statement of Lemma 8.2: lean weaker. The formula itself is stated literally, but only for two of the paper's three values of e. Lemma 8.2 fixes e ∈ {0,1,2} and (8.6) defines D_e = D_r + e/R, T_e f = R^{-e} I_c(R^e f), A_e f = R^{-e}(∂_R χ_m) J(R^e f). Lean states D_e T_e f = f − A_e f with the literal cutoff remainder at e = 0 (compactPrimitive_radial_identity, physicalGraphDeriv_physicalCompact_global, transport_compact_eq_sub_alias, and UniformFourierAlias.lean:152) and at e = 1 (streamGamma_eq_desired_sub_alias, and PhysicalMeanDomain.lean:1605). I found no declaration stating it at e = 2: a repository-wide search of NavierStokes/ for a right-hand side of the form `… - (compactAlias\|physicalAlias\|cutoffAlias\|exactAlias) …` returns exactly those seven lines, all e = 0 or e = 1. The paper does use the e = 2 case, in Corollary 8.5 (p. 95), where (D_r + 2/R)H_θ = −⟨E_θ⟩_Y + σ_θ ε∂_Z J_θ; there the source is independent of Y and has zero weighted radial integral, so by Lemma 8.2's last sentence the cutoff remainder vanishes. That degenerate case is covered in Lean by a different operator: SignedStressPrimitive.sigma P e F = −R^{-e} I_c(R^e F) (line 164) with a bump-density correction subtracted instead of the χ_m cutoff remainder, and physical_weighted_sigma_hasDerivAt gives (D_r + e/R)σ = −(F − bump) for every e, specialized at e = 2 by physical_angular_divergence (line 821, radialDivergence 2 σ = −adjusted; IntegratedMeanBalances.radialDivergence c f r = f'(r) + (c/r)f(r)). So the shortfall is the general-A_e form of (8.7) at e = 2, which is not used by the paper; the e = 0 and e = 1 statements are term-for-term the paper's, including the R^{-e} normalization and the (∂_R χ_m)·J shape of A_e. - **(8.10)**, p. 91, proof of Lemma 8.2: lean weaker. Same operator (the Fourier multiplier (2πi v_r·k)^{-1}, TorusInverse.multiplier) and the same shape of estimate, with the constant fixed before the source, but the derivative loss is larger and only p = 1 is stated as one declaration. inverse_finiteJets concludes a C^n bound on 𝓛^{-1}f from a C^{n+5} bound on f, where (8.10) at p = 1 asks for C^{m+4}: the extra order comes from the summable lattice majorant Σ_k weight(k)^{-4} (SmoothFourierData.summable_weight_inv_four, used through rapid_coefficient/coefficient_seminorm_bound with p+4 derivatives), against the paper's Σ_{k∈Z²}(1+\|k\|)^{-3}. No declaration was found for 𝓛^{-p} with the paper's single p+3 loss; higher p is reached by iterating one inverse at a time, which is why totalIntegral_finiteJets and exactAlias_finiteJets consume m + 6p input jets (5 per inverse plus 1 radial derivative per integration by parts) rather than the paper's ~2p+3. Two incidental differences in the Lean's favour: its inverse annihilates the zero Fourier mode by construction (multiplier d 0 = 0), so no zero-mean hypothesis on H is needed, and JetBound bounds joint parameter-and-torus jets, not only the paper's C^m_y (mixedJet_inverse_bound is the purely-y version, with the same loss 5). Lean: `NavierStokes.SmoothFamilyTorusInverse.inverse_finiteJets` (states, NavierStokes/SmoothFamilyTorusInverse.lean:865); `NavierStokes.ParametricTorusInverse.mixedJet_inverse_bound` (states, NavierStokes/ParametricTorusInverse.lean:654); `NavierStokes.UniformFourierAlias.realInverse_finiteJets` (states, NavierStokes/UniformFourierAlias.lean:611); `NavierStokes.TorusInverse.norm_multiplier_le` (implies, NavierStokes/TorusInverse.lean:260); `NavierStokes.UniformFourierAlias.totalIntegral_finiteJets` (uses, NavierStokes/UniformFourierAlias.lean:846); `NavierStokes.UniformFourierAlias.exactAlias_finiteJets` (uses, NavierStokes/UniformFourierAlias.lean:864).
- **(8.19)**, p. 95, statement of Lemma 8.6: lean weaker. The Fourier series matches term for term: TorusInverse.inverseCoeff multiplies the coefficient by (2πi·symbol .temporal k)⁻¹ with symbol .temporal k = v_t·k (symbol_formula) and vector .temporal = (√2−1, 1) = (b_g, 1), the paper's v_t from (6.2); the k = 0 term is 0 by inverseCoeff_zero, so the Lean sum over all of ℤ² equals the paper's sum over ℤ²\{0}. The existence/uniqueness claim is also stated (inverse_solves_smooth_torus, timeDerivative_unique). The norm bound differs by one derivative: the paper asserts C^m_y controlled by C^{m+4}_y, while every bound found in the Lean loses five torus derivatives. inverse_derivativeWord_bound bounds a word of m torus derivatives of the inverse by coeffSeminorm (m+1), and coefficient_seminorm_bound buys coeffSeminorm p from jets of order p+4, giving m+5; the packaged statements say so explicitly (mixedJet_inverse_bound: 'a mixed output jet with r torus derivatives requires only r+5 pure torus derivatives'; SmoothFamilyTorusInverse.inverse_finiteJets, read at line 865, requires JetBound f S (n+5) and its docstring says 'five torus derivatives lost'). The extra derivative comes from the Lean's choice of the summable majorant ∑ weight k ^(-4) (summable_weight_inv_four) where the paper's count uses (1+\|k\|)^{-3}, which is already summable in two dimensions; no '_three' majorant exists in the index. Partly offsetting, the Lean hypothesis is only on pure x- and pure y-derivative words (xJet of f and of swapFunction f) of that order, not on the full C^{m+5} norm. Rated low because what the later argument uses is a fixed finite derivative loss, and the M_α classes bound every fixed derivative order. - **display 98.1**, p. 98, proof of Lemma 8.7: different. Lean's coefficient matrix for the same two blocks is the unfactored moment matrix `LocalizedMomentRepair.matrix a l u = fun i j => ∫ t, t ^ a i * bump (l j) (u j) t`. Its exponent rows are the paper's exactly (angularPowers λ = ![2, -2-2λ, -2λ]; axialPowers λ = ![1, 1-2λ]), but two things of the display are absent from it. (i) In the Lemma 8.7 pipeline the entries are never evaluated to μ_p e^{jdp}: the profiles there are bumps on pairwise disjoint cells of the patch rather than e^{jd}-dilates of one η_0, their moments stay as the integrals `FiveProfileMoments.bumpMoment` (:151), and nonsingularity comes from `PowerMomentMatrix.intervalMomentMatrix_det_ne_zero` (:216) rather than from the displayed factorisation. The factorisation is not missing from the repository, only from this matrix: for the two-row outgoing-pulse instance of the very same `LocalizedMomentRepair.matrix`, `OutgoingPulseBounds.actual_matrix_entry` (:565) evaluates the entries to μ_{p_i} e^{(p_i+1)c_j}, `normalizedMatrix_entry` (:582) puts row i in the displayed shape μ_{p_i}·(1, e^{d p_i}), and `normalizedMatrix_det_ne_zero` (:618) proves the determinant nonzero by μ_0μ_1(e^{2β_0} − e^{2β_1}) ≠ 0 — the paper's own argument of dividing the rows by μ_p and reading the Vandermonde in the distinct e^{dp}. That is a 2×2 instance with the pulse exponents −(1/2+λ), −(1/2+2λ), so it is not a counterpart of A_θ (3×3, powers 2, −2−2λ, −2λ) or A_z (2×2, powers 1, 1−2λ); the paper's method is formalised, for other rows. (ii) The row constants 1, 2a, -a and 1, a are not in the Lean matrix: they are divided into the target vector instead (angularDebt C d = ![0, -(d 0)/(2C), d 2/C], axialDebt C d = ![0, -(d 1)/C]), with the amplitude C of `background lam C R = C * R ^ (-1 - 2λ)` (FiveRowRank.lean:110) playing a(η) of (8.24). I checked these constants against the Lean rows themselves: pressure_row (line 189) gives 2C ∫ R^{-2-2λ}Δv = -d₀, angular_row (206) gives C ∫ R^{1-2λ}γ = -d₁, axial_row (223) gives -C ∫ R^{-2λ}Δv = -d₂, matching the 2a, a, -a of the display. Since A_θ = diag(1, 2a, -a)·M and A_z = diag(1, a)·M', the two parametrisations determine the same correction; what the repository does not contain is the explicit matrix as displayed. The nonsingularity the paper reads off the Vandermonde is proved for the Lean matrix at arbitrary distinct exponents (matrix_det_ne_zero), which covers the paper's exponents for λ > 0 (angularPowers_injective, FiveRowRank.lean:84). Hence low, not medium. - **display 98.2**, p. 98, proof of Lemma 8.7: different. The first run and the second run agree on every fact; they differ only on whether the change of profile family counts against this display. The Lean follows the display's template clause by clause. (1) Coefficients: `coefficients = (matrix a l u)⁻¹.mulVec d` with powers angularPowers = ![2, -2-2λ, -2λ] and axialPowers = ![1, 1-2λ]. The paper's row factors (1, 2a, -a) and (1, a) are moved into the right-hand side: angularDebt C d = (0, -P/(2C), J_z/C) and axialDebt C d = (0, -J_θ/C). That repackaging is matrix algebra, given C ≠ 0. (2) The sums over j = 0,1,2 and j = 0,1 are `repair` over Fin 3 and Fin 2. (3) The prefactor q^{-A} and argument r/√q are `scaleField ell U` at ell = √q, U = q^{-A} (MeanRankUpdate.lean:1159-1162, and modelLength/modelVelocity at :544-545). (4) The q-normalised targets are `normalizeDebt (√q) (q^{-A}) d` = (q^{2A} P, q^{2A-3/2} J_θ, q^{2A-1} J_z), which are the powers of (8.17) as read from the p. 94 image. What differs is the object being combined. The paper's η_j are the dilates a_j^{-1} η_0(x/a_j), a_j = e^{jd}, and A_θ, A_z are the explicit matrices with entries μ_p e^{jdp} written just above the display. Lean's profiles are `bump (cellLower a b j) (cellUpper a b j)`: translates of one cutoff on equally spaced mesh cells, all of the same width and all with peak value 1 (bump_at_center, :37). No d > 0 makes these a dilated family, so the Lean family is not an instance of the paper's. The matrix Lean inverts has unevaluated integral entries and is not the paper's A_θ or A_z. Hence the u_j, s_j and the functions Δv_phys, γ_{d,phys} are different functions from the paper's, and neither formula follows from the other by formal steps. Severity is low. Lemma 8.7 is existential in the profiles, and its defining property (the five rows in physical variables) is proved for the Lean construction (physical_rows_on_patch :194, physical_rows_on_shaped_patch :1153); linearity in the targets is deltaVLinearMap (FiveRowRank.lean:348). On p. 98 the later text (Step 3 and Lemma 8.8) uses the lemma's properties, not the explicit coefficients; a text-layer search of pp. 90-110 found Δv_phys and γ_{d,phys} only on p. 98 (γ_{d,phys} also earlier, on p. 93). I did not read p. 99 onward as images. Lean also leaves the amplitude constant B free where the paper fixes 2^{1/2+λ} c_patch; that belongs to (8.24), not this display. - **display 99.2**, p. 99, proof of Lemma 8.8: lean weaker. The block is a two-column table (header 'term \| class') listing six class memberships for the terms of R_g in (8.26); the Lean counterpart is the five-step proof of radialRemainder_mem, whose subject radialRemainder (MeanIncrementBounds.lean:368) is term-for-term the right-hand side of (8.26) under the dictionary h.radial=Δβ, h.angular=Δv, h.axial=Δγ, b.radial=b, b.angular=V, b.axial=G, m=(β,v,γ), radialDiv 1 = D_r+1/R, viscosity 1 = ε(Δ₀-R⁻²), and IncrementBounds gives Δβ ∈ M_{H+1}, Δv,Δγ ∈ M_H with H=α. Five of the six rows agree exactly: row 2 at (H+2)-κ, row 3 at H+2, row 4 at 9/10+H, row 5 at H+H=M_{2α} (MeanIncrementBounds.lean:462, before it is weakened to H+9/10), row 6 at (H+1)+1-2κ; for rows 2 and 3 the Lean bounds the whole bracket, including the transport products the paper's table defers to the following sentence, so there it is at least as strong. Row 1 differs: the paper puts -t_*Δβ in M_{α+2}, justified by the preceding sentence 'All three increments are independent of the auxiliary variable. In particular, t_*Δβ = -ε∂_TΔβ'. The Lean step uses OperatorBounds.time (MeanIncrementBounds.lean:279), where time = slowTime + fastTime: slowTime gains +1, giving H+2, but fastTime gains 0, so the conjunction is only MeanClass s (H+1) — the paper's exponent for this row is not proved. The Lean assumes no auxiliary-independence of the increment here and keeps the fast-time derivative deliberately (docstring at MeanIncrementBounds.lean:473-474: 'This includes fast time on the radial stream'); no gr-side variant that subtracts the fast-time term exists, unlike thetaResidual_change_sub_fast_mem and axialResidual_change_sub_fast_mem for the other two components. The weaker row still suffices for the target exponent of the lemma, since H + 9/10 - 2κ ≤ H + 1 whenever κ ≥ 0 (the mono_exponent at MeanLocalDefectBounds.lean:82 is exactly this), so radialRemainder_mem and hence the gain of Lemma 8.8 are proved; only this row's stated class is weaker than the manuscript's. - **(9.5)**, p. 104, statement of Proposition 9.3: different. The paper's (9.5) is a physical statement: the Cartesian space-time jet norm (5.23) of the flat field F^[j] (base error + pulse-cutoff tails + cutoff remainders, p. 103) is at most C_{j,m,N} q^N for every N, and the next sentence says the bound is uniform over bands, labels and points. No Lean declaration states this, and the repository defines no physical field for the flat part alone. What it states is flatness in the normalized chart, one theorem per summand of `errors.total = base + gaussian + aliasError`, each for every real α: `baseError_native` (gain h·α, loss 0), `gaussian_field_native` (gain h·α, loss 2·h·m, under hN), `axis_native` with `alias_eq_lift` (gain h·α, loss 0). Each is a bound A·Q_n^(h·α − loss m)·S_n^p on chart derivatives of order ≤ m for bands n ≥ 4, and each is a `NativeBounds` on the OPEN strip domain (or its lift); `NativeBounds.smooth` and `.bounds` say nothing off that open set. The hypotheses are met by the construction (`state_invariant`). Where the display does follow by formal steps: at physical points whose profile radius is strictly inside the active interval. There the chart point lies in the open lifted strip (`movingStrip_domain` is an iff with Ioo; the strict radius bound is algebra from the stated squared-radius identity), so `native_lift`, `NativeBounds.weaken`, `excluded_nativeBounds` give one bound for `errors.total` at any α (exactly lines 861-872 of `native_residual` with α in place of 1/2+σ), `SmoothNear.of_open` gives the smoothness hypothesis, `cartesianPull_jet_bound` (stated for an arbitrary chart field) converts to C·q^(gain − loss) with the S_n^e factor absorbed, and taking α large is exponent arithmetic. Where it does not: at the two radial endpoints of the active shell. `active` is defined with a closed interval Icc, and `actual_selectedGeometry` puts the chart image of a shell point only in `nativeDomain` and in the closure of the lifted strip (`selected_polar_closure`), never in the open strip. At such a point `cartesianPull_jet_bound` needs `SmoothNear (errors.total n)` at the chart point and the jet bound there; the repository's tool for the latter, `jet_bound_at_closure`, needs `ContDiffAt ℝ ∞` at the edge point. The repository states that smoothness across the edge only for the full residual (`realization_native_smooth`) and uses it only there (`selected_residual_jet_bound`, lines 939-945). For the flat part I found none: `axisFlat`/`gaussianFlat` are classes on `G.strip`, the invariant's `phase` is smooth on `G.strip.domain` only, `fullResidual_decomposition` holds only on the open lifted strip (so smoothness of `errors.total` cannot be read off the full residual), and a search of decls.jsonl signatures and of the sources for ContDiff/smooth statements about `axisymmetricAlias`, the alias states, `errors.gaussian`, `errors.aliasError` or `errors.total` returned nothing. (Gaussian coefficients are smooth on the larger `G.domain` by `gaussianCoefficientSmooth`, but nothing comparable is stated for the alias.) Regularity is an analytic fact under the decision rule, so the display as written (all points) is not a formal consequence. In the other direction the Lean statements do not follow from the display either: they are per summand and in chart scales, which a physical bound on the sum does not give. So the two are not comparable, and the Lean reaches the surrounding result by another route: it never forms a physical flat bound, but folds the flat terms into the full residual at the fixed gain h·(1/2+σ) and converts only the full residual to physical form, edges included (`residual_jetRate`). Smaller points: the Lean bounds are for bands n ≥ 4 (q below a fixed scale), and outside the active shell only the base error has a stated physical all-power bound (`base_exterior_jetRate`). Severity low: the paper uses (9.5) once later, to supply E_{j,m} in (9.18) (p. 114); the Lean obtains the corresponding full-residual rate without a separate flat term. Not verified: that no smoothness statement for the stage-j alias near the edge exists under a name or shape my searches missed; the index line of `native_residual` is 845 (861 is the cited step). - **display 104.2**, p. 104, proof of Proposition 9.3: different. Both bounds are reached, but by other formulas. (i) Integral bound: the Lean states an exact factorization in place of the paper's inequality. exists_smooth_factor_original is the paper's own left-hand side, ∫_0^x e^(-c/u²) u^(-j) b(u) du, in the paper's own exponential and real-power notation, equal to e^(-c/x²) x^(3-j) F(x) with F smooth on all of ℝ and F(0) = b(0)/(2c); the witness is the explicitly constructed F = factor c j b (exists_smooth_factor), so the constant is exhibited, not a limit value. Since F is continuous it is bounded on [0,δ₀], which gives the paper's C_{M,a} δ^(3-M) e^(-a/δ²) on any bounded range of δ, and the paper states its bound for sufficiently small positive δ. The Lean exponent is a natural number j rather than the paper's real M ≥ 0, but real M is recovered in one line by absorbing the surplus power rather than discarding it: take j = ⌈M⌉ ≥ M; for 0 < s ≤ δ, s^(-M) = s^(-j) · s^(j-M) ≤ s^(-j) δ^(j-M) since j - M ≥ 0, and the integrand is positive, so ∫_0^δ s^(-M) e^(-a/s²) ds ≤ δ^(j-M) ∫_0^δ s^(-j) e^(-a/s²) ds = δ^(j-M) · e^(-a/δ²) δ^(3-j) F(δ) = F(δ) · δ^(3-M) e^(-a/δ²), with F = factor a j 1 continuous (factor_contDiff, FlatPrimitiveFactor.lean:255) hence bounded on [0, δ₀] — the same routine-boundedness step as in the integer case. No M-restriction therefore remains on the integral half of the block; the 3 - j in the conclusion is itself a real power (scale_eq_rpow). The paper's integrand is the j-th inverse power alone (b ≡ 1); the Lean carries a general smooth coefficient b, which is more general. For j = 3, b ≡ 1 the bound is an exact identity (primitive_three_one). (ii) Derivative bound: iteratedDeriv_polynomialEdge gives the exact structure d^k/ds^k e^(-a/s²) = q_k(1/s) e^(-a/s²) with q_k = jetPolynomial a 1 k, and each step of derivativePolynomial raises the inverse-power degree by 3, so the paper's sharp s^(-3k) is implicit in it; but no declaration computes natDegree (jetPolynomial c p m), and the packaged inequality polynomialEdge_jets_bound uses one uniform non-sharp exponent N = jetOrder c p n = Σ_{i≤n} natDegree (jetPolynomial c p i) (= 3n(n+1)/2 for p = 1) for all i ≤ n in place of 3k. - **display 112.1**, p. 112, proof of Lemma 9.7: different. No declaration defines a function D_{v,a}(m) indexed by a source vertex a, and nothing in the repository builds a DAG of the correction construction. The same bookkeeping is carried out twice, each time inside one inductive expression type rather than once for the whole construction. (i) GenericDifferentialPolynomial.Expression has nodes input / coeff / add / mul / directional — the paper's "sums, products, derivatives" — but no node for the "proved inverses" the paper also lists as graph operations, and no node of general weight d_{v,w}: a derivative node adds exactly one order, so an operation costing d derivatives is d stacked nodes. Its ApproximationRates.threshold : ℕ → ℕ propagates by the paper's recursion: directional gives threshold m = a.threshold (m+1) (the edge weight d_{v,w} = 1), add gives max of the two children at the same m (d_{v,w} = 0), a coeff node gives 0, matching the initial value D_{b,a}(m) = 0 for b ≠ a, and in polynomial_jetRate_of_stages the input nodes are given threshold = id, matching D_{a,a}(m) = m. mul takes sup over k ≤ m of each child's threshold rather than the child's value at m; every threshold built here (id, 0, max, m ↦ θ(m+1), and sup over k ≤ m) is monotone, so that sup equals the child's value at m and the two agree, the Lean version being the safe choice for a non-monotone threshold. Two things do differ from the display. The propagated quantity is literally the approximation-stage index J required for the tail estimate at jet order m (tail_rate : ∀ m J, threshold m ≤ J → …), not a count of derivatives of an input field; the two are identified only through the hypothesis htail (m ≤ J) of polynomial_jetRate_of_stages, which is what forces threshold = id at the sources. And one function is carried instead of one per source a: the maximum over sources is taken as the recursion proceeds, so what is computed is max_a D_{v,a}(m), the coarsening the paper's conclusion uses. (ii) FlatKernelBounds.Expr.jetOrder is the same recursion for the kernel expressions: k at a jet k source node, 0 at every non-source node, max over the children at add and mul; there the m + d_{v,w} shift is performed by rewriting the expression with Expr.diff, which sends jet k to jet (k+1), instead of by shifting the counting function's argument. Neither construction is applied to the correction-stage graph of Lemma 9.7. In that chain the finiteness of the derivative counts is not needed as a separate step, because every stage field is asserted smooth of infinite order (ActualCandidateAssembly.stages_smooth : ∀ j, ContDiffOn ℝ ∞ (potentialStages …) (physicalDomain …), and likewise for directStages and pressureStages, lines 879-889) and the per-order estimates are stated with the derivative order m quantified in front of the constant (WholeDomainStageBounds.actual_finite_residual_bound). So the paper's formula as written was not checked; what was checked in its place is the same recursion for two restricted classes of expressions, plus infinite-order smoothness of the stage fields. - **display 113.2**, p. 113, proof of Lemma 9.8: lean weaker. The Lean bound charges (1+h) for every Cartesian derivative of the physical phase, spatial ones included: for total order i = a+b it gives Q^(-i(1+h)) = Q^(-(a+b)(1+h)), where the paper gives Q^(-a/2 - b(1+h)). Since Q ≤ 1 and h ≥ 0, -(a+b)(1+h) ≤ -a/2 - b(1+h) with equality only when a = 0, so the Lean factor is the larger loss whenever a ≥ 1: the Lean statement is implied by the paper's, not conversely. The hypothesis a+b ≥ 1 matches (`1 ≤ i`), the slow factor S_*^{P_{a,b}} matches (`S n ^ ((d+1)*i)`, a power allowed to grow with the order), the constant depends only on the order and on fixed data, and the geometric-in-i form of the Lean bound is a strengthening in shape, not in the Q power. The loss is not recovered elsewhere: the same uniform (1+h) is what `common_jets` proves for the graph composition and what every consumer uses. - **display 113.4**, p. 113, proof of Lemma 9.8: lean weaker. The oscillatory factor matches exactly: `character ((carrier h n)*(j)) Φ = exp(i·k·m·Φ)` with `carrier` the rounded integer frequency k and j the harmonic m, and the constant is allowed to depend on the order and on a bound H for \|j\|, as the paper's C_{a,b,m} does. The Q exponent differs: the Lean charges `derivativeCost h = s_t = 1 + 3h/2` for each of the m Cartesian derivatives whatever their direction, giving Q^(-m s_t) in place of the paper's Q^(-a s_x - b s_t) with m = a+b. Since s_x = 1/2 + h/2 < s_t = 1 + 3h/2 for h ≥ 0 and Q ≤ 1, the Lean factor is the larger loss whenever a ≥ 1, so the Lean bound follows from the paper's and not conversely. The slow factor S_*^{P_{a,b,m}} matches (`S n ^ (d+e*m)`). Severity is low because the paper itself collapses to the worst case one paragraph later: its ℓ_m = 2A+(m+1)s_t (eq-p114-1) charges s_t for every derivative, and that is exactly what the Lean's `physicalLoss` reaches. - **display 118.1**, p. 118, proof of Proposition 10.1: different. The paper chooses a cutoff by two properties tied to parameters: r_0 is any fixed positive number, z_0 is chosen with C_0(τ_0 + z_0^{1/D}) < q_*/2, the cutoff is 1 on {r ≤ r_0/2, \|z\| ≤ z_0/2}, and its support is compactly inside the open cylinder {r < r_0, \|z\| < z_0}. The Lean fixes one explicit cutoff with absolute constants, cutoff(16 r²)·cutoff(4 z) with a bump of inner radius 1/2 and outer radius 1. What is stated about it: it is 1 on {r² < 1/32, \|z\| < 1/8} (and, by one application of cutoff_one_of_abs_le, on the closed version), and its closed support lies in the closed cylinder {r² ≤ 1/16, \|z\| ≤ 1/4}. Three things in the display are not supplied. (1) No single pair (r_0, z_0) makes the display follow from these statements: the plateau clause in z needs z_0 ≤ 1/4 from what is stated, while compact containment in {\|z\| < z_0} needs z_0 > 1/4, since cutoff_tsupport gives closed z-support reaching ±1/4. The Lean statement is containment in the closed cylinder, not compact containment in the open one. (2) The radial geometry differs: the Lean plateau radius is 1/(4√2) against support radius 1/4, not the paper's ratio 2, so the Lean plateau lemma does not follow from the display with r_0 = 1/4 either. (3) The freedom in r_0 and the smallness of z_0 relative to q_* have no counterpart: no file mentions both spatialCutoff/supportCylinder and qstar (repository grep: the two file lists are disjoint), and physical_q_bound, the counterpart of (10.3), is not applied to the cutoff. The Lean does not need the margin from q = q_*, because its local potential, direct field and pressure are stated smooth on all of {t < 1} (LocalPaper.Properties), so any fixed compact cylinder is admissible. The parts of the sentence around the display that do match are smoothness in (r², z), 0 ≤ χ_x ≤ 1, axisymmetry, compact support, and being 1 on an open neighbourhood of the origin. The conclusions of Proposition 10.1 are proved in local_theorem_with_compact_candidate with the open plateau as the neighbourhood, so the surrounding result stands. I did not verify lemma by lemma that the Lean bump is strictly below 1 outside its inner ball; point (1) only uses that no declaration states a larger z-plateau. - **display 118.2**, p. 118, proof of Proposition 10.1: different. No τ_0 makes the Lean switch an instance of the paper's χ_t: timeSwitch vanishes on \|t\| ≤ 3/8, i.e. for 1 - t ≥ 5/8, which needs τ_0 ≥ 5/8, while it is known to equal 1 only for t ≥ 3/4, i.e. 1 - t ≤ 1/4, which needs τ_0/2 ≥ 1/4, so τ_0 ≤ 1/2 — and the paper additionally demands τ_0 < 1/2. The Lean switch is also only shown to vanish on a two-sided neighbourhood of 0 (\|t\| ≤ 3/8) rather than on all of 1 - t ≥ τ_0, i.e. nothing here rules out t < -3/8. The product c(x,t) = χ_x(x)χ_t(t) is never formed as a single function in Lean: the two cutoffs are applied in sequence (TimeLocalization.activatedVelocity applied to the spatially cut field), which is the same product pointwise. Rated low because both consequences the paper's proof draws from χ_t are stated for the Lean switch — the fields vanish near t = 0 (used for the initial-rest clause) and the switch is 1 on the late interval where the paper's and Lean's fields must agree (t ∈ [3/4, 1), as in PaperLocalization.local_theorem_with_compact_candidate) — and the numeric gap between 5/8 and 1/2 is a different admissible choice of constants, not a weaker statement, since the Lean never needs the support of c to sit inside the paper's Ω_*. - **display 118.4**, p. 118, section 10.1: absent. No declaration was found that states this Poisson equation with a force-divergence term on the right. The closest asset, and a structural analogue of this very display, is `NavierStokes.R3WeakPressure.momentumGradient_divergence` (NavierStokes/R3WeakPressure.lean:120-126): with `momentumGradient U V G i = Δ(U i) - ∂_t (U i) + V i - ∑_j ∂_j (G i j)` (same file, :88-91), V occupying the force slot and G the velocity stress, it concludes `divergence (momentumGradient U V G) = -(∑_i ∑_j ∂_i ∂_j (G i j))` — the paper's identity with `div f` dropped, because it assumes `hV : divergence V = 0` (:124). That hypothesis is exactly what the paper's following sentence flags as possibly failing ("the force may have nonzero divergence"). In the downstream statements I opened, the force slot is filled by the literal zero function: `R3DifferenceStress.momentum_equation` (NavierStokes/R3DifferenceStress.lean:110) and `R3LocalizedPressure.localized_pressure_gradient` (NavierStokes/R3LocalizedPressure.lean:126) both write `functionMomentum U (fun _ _ => 0) G`, and `smooth_pressure_gradient` (NavierStokes/R3SmoothPressure.lean:124-142) carries the same `dV : functionDivergence V = 0` through to `pressure_gradient_unique` (:161), which is the only consumer of `momentumGradient_divergence` that I found. The force-free pressure identity itself is `R3PressureFourier.stressPressure_poisson` (NavierStokes/R3PressureFourier.lean:363): `Δ (stressPressure g) = -∑_i ∑_j ∂_i ∂_j (g i j)`, again with no force term. `NavierStokesR3.ConservativeDifference.weak_pressure_poisson` (NavierStokes/R3/ConservativeDifference.lean:431) is a third, further-off relative: a weak identity tested against ψ ∈ C_c^∞ rather than the pointwise display, written for the difference w = u - v of two fields whose residuals are equal, so the two forces cancel and no `- div f` appears; it belongs to the comparison argument of Lemma 10.5 (paper p.122, display Δπ_* = -∑ ∂_i ∂_j g_ij), not to the §10.1 localization. So the picture is not that the repository never writes a pressure Poisson equation, but that every version of it I found assumes the force (or the difference of forces) is divergence free, which is the one feature the p.118 display exists to record. My searches (listed in `searched`) over the index and over the `NavierStokes/` tree found no declaration taking the spatial divergence of `navierStokesResidual`, and no identity retaining a divergence-of-the-force term; I did not read all 815 files. The paper's own derivation is three lines from (10.5) plus div u = 0 (div ∂_t u = 0, div Δu = Δ div u = 0, div((u·∇)u) = ∑ ∂_i ∂_j (u_i u_j), div ∇p = Δp), but that derivation is not carried out in Lean for these fields. Severity low: the display is terminal in the paper — it carries no equation number, and "Poisson" occurs exactly once in the whole text layer, in the sentence immediately after it — and I found no later step of §10 or of the Lean development that uses it, so Proposition 10.1 and Lemma 10.2 are unaffected. - **display 119.1**, p. 119, proof of Lemma 10.2: different. The paper's display is a mean-value inequality for one mixed component, in the time direction only, with the right side equal to \|t'-t\| times the supremum of the single next time derivative over K_0 x [t,t']. The repository has no declaration stating it. The nearest counterpart is a step inside a proof: hLip at GenericEndpointExtension.lean:461, a Lipschitz bound for the whole n-th joint derivative tensor on an open convex set s, with constant max C 0 where C is any bound for the norm of the (n+1)-st tensor on all of s. The enclosing theorem's statement is only uniform continuity of the n-th tensor on s; it carries no constant and no increment. The two formulas do not follow from one another by formal steps. From hLip, taking two points with the same space coordinate and extracting a component gives the paper's left side bounded by \|t'-t\| times the larger quantity sup over s of the full (n+1)-tensor norm, not by the paper's right side. In the other direction the paper's time-only, single-component inequality does not give a Lipschitz bound in all space-time directions. Obtaining the display as written needs the mean-value inequality itself, which the repository uses from Mathlib inside proofs but does not state in this form. What the paper does with the display is proved: the next sentences only use that the right side is bounded independently of t, t', so each derivative is uniformly Cauchy as t increases to 1; the Lean gets this from actualJet_uniformContinuousOn through exists_limit_of_uniformContinuousOn (lines 481-482, 488-489), and OffplaneJetExtensions.extension_of_local_jets (line 113, using the extension at line 122) turns it into one-sided extensions that ActualEndpointInputs.endpointInputs_of_representations consumes for the potential, direct and pressure stage fields. In the Lean the step is applied to a bump-localized field on the strip (-1,1) x Space, not to G on K_0. - **(10.12)**, p. 120, proof of Lemma 10.3: different. The shape agrees — a power of b_j times a constant depending on j and the derivative order — but three things differ. (i) Lean's power is b^k / b^j, where k is the TOTAL order of the joint (t,x) derivative tensor; for \|α\| > 0 that is b_j^{\|α\|+m-j}, not the paper's b_j^{m-j}, and since b_j ≥ 1 it is a larger right-hand side than the paper writes. (iii) Lean's left-hand side is the localized term, carrying an extra spatial bump spatialCutoff m (equal to 1 on ‖x‖ < m+1), which is what makes the sup finite with no global bound on the jets; the paper gets finiteness instead from supp F_j ⊂ 𝒦. I did not check an implication in either direction between C_{j,m}‖F_j‖_{C^{\|α\|}} and templateBound ha m j k. Severity low rather than medium because the only use the paper makes of (10.12) — the 2^{-j} tail after the choice of b_j — is proved in Lean from this bound (localized_derivative_tail_bound, line 236). - **display 120.1**, p. 120, proof of Lemma 10.3: different. Same device, a different formula. Lean takes no minimum over a constraint set: localScale j is an arbitrary natural above 2^j · boundSum j, produced by Classical.choose from the Archimedean property (exists_nat_gt), and boundSum j = Σ_{m<j} Σ_{k<j} templateBound m j k SUMS the template constants instead of maximizing C_{j,m} b^{m-j}‖F_j‖_{C^a}; the paper's b^{m-j} factor is not inside Lean's defining inequality at all, it is used afterwards through power_ratio_le (b^k/b^j ≤ 1/b for k < j). The paper's requirement b_j > b_{j-1} is then imposed separately by scale = DiagonalScale.doublingEnvelope localScale, which is strictly monotone and in addition at least doubles at each step (2 * scale j ≤ scale (j+1)) — a condition the paper does not ask for. The conclusion the paper draws from its choice, that the quantity bounded in (10.12) is at most 2^{-j}, is localized_derivative_tail_bound, but over the index range m < j and k < j (spatial window index and total joint derivative order) rather than the paper's a + m ≤ ⌊j/2⌋; both ranges exhaust every fixed order as j grows, which is all the tail argument needs. Lean also has no b_0 = 1 normalization: doublingEnvelope at 0 is max 1 (localScale 0). - **display 122.5**, p. 122, proof of Lemma 10.5: different. The paper's two bounds are in the repository, as one additive statement, with the same exponent 3/2 and the same right-hand sides, but with phi^5 rather than phi^4 on the left. `R3WeightedLp.stress_weight_five` (R3WeightedLp.lean:191) states, for a stress g with ‖g x‖ <= W x ^ 2 + 2 (U x W x) — the paper's w_i w_j and w_i u_j terms, with W = \|w\| and U = \|u\| — that lpNorm (weightedNorm phi g) (3/2) <= lpNorm (fourthWeight phi W) 6 * lpNorm W 2 + 2 (lpNorm U 6 * lpNorm W 2), where `weightedNorm phi g = phi^5 ‖g‖` (R3PressureCommutator.lean:31) and `fourthWeight phi W = phi^4 W`, so lpNorm (fourthWeight phi W) 6 is exactly B_R. The first summand is the paper's B_R ‖w‖_2, linear in B_R, and the second is the paper's ‖w‖_2 ‖u‖_6 with a factor 2 from the two cross terms w_i u_j + u_i w_j; both come from the paper's own Holder step 6 + 2 -> 3/2 (`holder_six_two_three_halves`, R3WeightedLp.lean:163, applied at :222-223 through `lpNorm_mul_le`, :42). The single difference is the cutoff power on the left: phi^5 against the paper's phi^4, introduced by `fifthQuadratic_le` (:114, a^5 b^2 <= (a^4 b) b) because the pressure commutator consumes precisely the phi^5 norm (`errorBound`, R3PressureCutoff.lean:158-162, and the hypothesis hw of `commutatorLp`, :76). I checked the direction: phi takes values in [0,1] (Cutoff.range, R3PressureCommutator.lean:23), so phi^5 <= phi^4 pointwise, and therefore the Lean statement follows from the paper's two bounds plus the triangle inequality, while the paper's does not follow from the Lean's. The Lean's conclusion is the weaker of the two; it is what the Lean's own pressure chain needs, and the surrounding result is proved with it. Separately, the `NavierStokes/R3/` subtree carries an unrelated L^{6/5} family (`weighted_tensorDiff_bound`, LocalizedTensorBounds.lean:170, with phi^2 on the left and the interpolated ‖w‖_2^{3/2} B_R^{1/2}) for its dual route; that is not a counterpart of this display, and the sublinearity in B_R there is a property of that route, not of the repository's statement of this formula. - **(10.19)**, p. 123, proof of Lemma 10.5: different. Two declarations in the repository bound the cutoff pressure flux, and neither is the paper's expression; they differ from it, and from each other, in the Hölder split. (i) The live route. exists_uniform_actual_pressure_flux_bound gives CP * ((B^{1/2}+1)(A_R/R + R^{-2}) + R^{-7/4}B_R^{3/4}). Its second term matches the paper exactly: R^{-7/4}B_R^{3/4} is the paper's R^{-3/4}B_R^{3/4} multiplied by the R^{-1} the paper carries outside (PressureFlux.rpow_three_fourths_div, R^{-3/4}/R = R^{-7/4}), and that is the commutator contribution. Its first term is not the paper's. The paper pairs the localised tensor in L^{3/2} against the Riesz piece in L^3 and then substitutes (10.17) to turn ‖∇(φ_R^4 w)‖_2 into A_R, producing R^{-1}(B_R+1)B_R^{1/2}, in which B_R and ‖w‖_2 are the only quantities; the Lean pairs in L^{6/5} against L^6 and applies Sobolev to the Riesz test function instead (riesz_rTest_bound), producing (B^{1/2}+1)(A_R/R + R^{-2}): the dissipation root A_R appears explicitly and undivided, and a separate R^{-2} term appears where the paper has none. Using (10.17), B_R ≤ C(A_R + R^{-1}‖w‖_2), the Lean's first two summands dominate the paper's R^{-1}B_R^{3/2} up to constants; the factor (B^{1/2}+1) in the Lean and (B_R+1)B_R^{1/2} in the paper differ in the power of B_R carried. (ii) A second, structurally closer bound that the main theorem does not use. NavierStokes/R3StressPressureEstimate.lean bounds one (i,j) component of the Gaussian-regularized flux, ‖∫ fderiv ℝ (φ^8) x (w x) * regularized n i j g x‖ ≤ rawBound R L M V B, where B is lpNorm (fourthWeight φ ‖w‖) 6 = ‖φ_R^4 w‖_6 = B_R exactly, M bounds ‖w‖_2, V bounds the L², L⁶ and L^∞ norms of the comparison field, and the stress hypothesis ‖g x‖ ≤ ‖w x‖² + 2‖u x‖‖w x‖ is the paper's. rawBound_le then gives, for R ≥ 1, rawBound ≤ coefficient(L,M,V)/R * (B_R^{3/2} + B_R + 1) — an R^{-1} prefactor times a polynomial in B_R with no A_R, which is the paper's shape rather than the pressureEnvelope shape, and whose leading term R^{-1}B_R^{3/2}M^{1/2} is the paper's R^{-1}(B_R+1)B_R^{1/2} leading term. The commutator part still differs from the paper, and by a third split: rawBound's error terms are R^{-1}·R^{-1/2}·nearConstant·(B_R M + 2VM) and R^{-1}·R^{-3/2}·farConstant·(M²+2VM), i.e. R^{-3/2}B_R and R^{-5/2}, where the paper has R^{-7/4}B_R^{3/4} — the near kernel is taken in L^{6/5} and the remainder in L² (R3PressureNearKernel) rather than the single L^{4/3} commutator kernel of the p.122 display; and rawBound_le then throws the extra decay away (R^{-1/2}, R^{-3/2} ≤ 1 for R ≥ 1), so the stated conclusion R^{-1}(B_R^{3/2}+B_R+1) is, for R ≥ 1, a strictly larger majorant than the paper's bracket up to constants. It also covers the regularized flux of a single component, not ∫ π w·∇χ_R itself. I grepped NavierStokes/ for `import NavierStokes.R3StressPressureEstimate` and found no file importing it; the atlas likewise records the module as an orphan not imported by the umbrella build. Two things that look like differences are not: the Lean quantifies over R ≥ 1 in both (i) and (ii), which is also the paper's range (p. 121-122, "For R ≥ 1, we write φ_R(x) = φ(x/R), χ_R = φ_R^8"), and the Lean's CP, built from the uniform bounds M (energy), U (the L³ norm of the compact field) and G₀ (the L¹ norm of the tensor difference), has the dependence the paper allows its C_T (p. 121: "may depend on T and the stated bounds for the fields, but not on the cutoff radius"). Rated low rather than medium because the Lean's envelope is the one its own closure consumes: eq_of_pressure_flux_bound takes exactly pressureEnvelope as hypothesis and returns u = v on [0,T], and classical_uniqueness_on_Icc (WholeSpaceUniqueness.lean:60-68) feeds the one into the other, so Lemma 10.5's conclusion is proved; what is not checked is the paper's (10.19) as written. Substituting the (10.22) dilation a = √ν, b = ν^{-1/2} (the arguments rescale_candidate passes, with ν > 0) gives ‖a‖² = ν and \|(b³)⁻¹\| = ν^{3/2}, hence factor ν^{5/2} — exactly the paper's; Lean's kineticEnergy carries a 1/2 that the paper's ‖·‖₂² does not, but it multiplies both sides and cancels, and Lean's time argument comes first. So this half is a faithful general statement awaiting instantiation. Second equality (dissipation): no declaration was found relating ν∫₀¹‖∇u_ν‖₂² dt to ν^{5/2}∫₀¹‖∇u‖₂² dt, or any scaling law for CompactEnergy.dissipation u t = ∑_i ∫ ‖∂_i u(t,x)‖² dx. For the concrete pulse corrections the Lean proves the log-coordinate analogue that the paper's next sentence anticipates: correctionJet is ∂_y^k of δV(e^y), i.e. (X∂_X)^k, bounded by C·exp(-1/(4λ))·(1+\|amp η\|) with C independent of λ (paper_correction_bounds) — the bumps there have fixed width in log X, so no ρ^{-r} arises — and only the first η-derivative is bounded (correctionJet_eta_bound), not all k. The amplitude enters as an additive factor (1 + \|amp η\|) rather than as the multiplicative v_*. Passing from the (X∂_X)^k bounds to the display's ∂_X^r ρ^{-r} form, and iterating η-derivatives past the first, is not carried out anywhere I found. The sentence right after the display says these choices are made on a slightly larger compact interval I containing [-1,1]; every Lean statement of the cut that I found (all hits of `99 / 100 < ... chi`) quantifies eta over Icc (-1) 1. The repository has an enlarged interval (window = [-11/10, 11/10]) for the analytic coefficient space, but no declaration asserts the cut on it or on any interval larger than [-1,1]; ProfileFamily.slope is stated on the open window only conditionally on chi >= 99/100. Extending the cut beyond [-1,1] is not a formal step: the Lean proof takes the compact minimum of H^2 over Icc (-1) 1 intersected with {\|Z\| <= j/10}, root uniqueness is stated only on Icc (-1) 1, and PressureData gives the pressure sign conditions only on Icc (-1) 1. (By my own hand computation, not checked in Lean, the cubic H has further real zeros near eta = +-1.06, inside the Lean window, so the cut on that window would need a separate lower bound on \|Z\| there.) What the Lean states follows from the display by restricting eta to [-1,1]. Later use in the paper: the two regions of (B.2) are used in the proof of Proposition B.3 (pp. 149-150, endpoint inequality (B.19), on the rectangle [0,4.1] x [-1,1]) and in Lemma B.4 (p. 151, on [X0,Xi] x [-1,1]); the Section 4 restatement on p. 37 does not mention I. I found no place where the paper uses chi > .99 at an eta outside [-1,1]; the interval I is used for the coefficient norm (B.4) and the approximation (B.13), which involve chi as a function, not the cut. Hence low severity. Lean: `NavierStokes.NaturalAxisData.chi` (defines, NavierStokes/NaturalAxisData.lean:38); `NavierStokes.NaturalAxisRange.exists_cutoff_parameters` (states, NavierStokes/NaturalAxisRange.lean:212); `NavierStokes.NaturalAxisRange.ideal_prefix_cutoff_parameters` (states, NavierStokes/NaturalAxisRange.lean:220); `NavierStokes.NaturalAxisRange.exists_sigma` (states, NavierStokes/NaturalAxisRange.lean:193); `NavierStokes.NaturalAxisRange.low_Z_has_H_margin` (implies, NavierStokes/NaturalAxisRange.lean:173); `NavierStokes.NaturalAxisData.PressureData` (uses, NavierStokes/NaturalAxisData.lean:192); `NavierStokes.SchedulePressure.natural_profileFamily` (states, NavierStokes/SchedulePressure.lean:252); `NavierStokes.NaturalAxisCoefficients.window` (uses, NavierStokes/NaturalAxisCoefficients.lean:27); `NavierStokes.NaturalProfile.ProfileFamily` (uses, NavierStokes/NaturalProfile.lean:559); `NavierStokes.NaturalExitBounds.exists_exit` (uses, NavierStokes/NaturalExitBounds.lean:155). - **(B.20)**, p. 149, proof of Proposition B.3: different. No declaration was found stating (B.20) in its own shape, i.e. a bound on \|𝒟_X log Φ\| + \|H_* ∂_η log Φ\| that shrinks like Cχ + C/Λ; no statement anywhere combines χ with √χ or with a C/Λ error of this form. Both quantities (B.20) bounds are nonetheless bounded in Lean — by fixed constants rather than by Cχ + C/Λ. `Estimates.p1_positive_upper` (NaturalExitBounds.lean:138) states 0 < p1 f (X,η) ≤ B 0, and p1 f p = -2·p.1·partialY f p / f p (NaturalEntrance.lean:31) is -2·𝒟_X log Φ once f = angularProfile a Λ Φ, the η-amplitude cancelling in a radial logarithmic derivative; `Estimates.parameter_jets` (NaturalExitBounds.lean:145-150) states \|∂_η^m log φ(ΛX,·)\| ≤ B m for every m, the case m = 1 being the η-log-derivative of (B.20). The constants B m are fixed before Λ (`exists_exit`, line 155, chooses B first) and do not shrink with χ or with 1/Λ, so the smallness the paper extracts from (B.20) is not what these declarations supply. The Lean reaches the same place by another route. Where the paper makes the two logarithmic derivatives quantitatively small (O(χ) + O(1/Λ)) so that they can be absorbed into ΛLχ, the Lean (i) keeps them unestimated inside the explicit remainder `sourceRemainder` (NaturalEntrance.lean:282), whose Y- and η-log-derivative slots are divided only by a profile clamped below by 1/8; (ii) proves instead that the remainder's χ-dependent pieces *vanish* at χ = 0 — `chi_zero_imp_H_zero` kills the H ∂_η log Φ term and `reference_phiY_zero` (a consequence of the exact identity `deriv_profile`, whose right-hand side carries the explicit factor χ/2) kills the Y-derivative term — and (iii) converts that vanishing into the absorption by compactness of the parameter set (`reference_source_absorption`, via `compact_absorption` at line 86) rather than by an explicit constant C. The C/Λ part of (B.20) is replaced by `sourceRemainder_uniform_limit`, which makes the whole coefficient-space correction smaller than 1/10 for Λ large. The paper reuses (B.20) in two later places and I traced both. (a) p. 150, proof of Lemma B.4 ('We use its gradient bounds (B.20) and (B.21) and source bound (B.17)'): the source half of (B.23) is `ReferenceBounds.sourceQ_uniform_threshold` (ReferenceBounds.lean:1148), whose 47/50·LΛχ + 12/5 is the paper's .94LΛχ + 2.4; its only hypothesis on the η-log-derivative is the bounded jet \|parameterPartial P.f p / P.f p - Λ·realGradient h j σ p.2\| ≤ B (collected by `qJets_bound`, line 635), and it is proved from the exact identity `sourceQ_model` (line 606) plus compactness (`qModel_uniform_lower`, line 460, through `compact_absorption`), with no Cχ + C/Λ bound anywhere. The hold-region analogue runs the same way (`ActivationContinuation.hold_source_identity` line 327, `holdModel_chi_zero` line 283, `holdModel_uniform_lower` line 292 via `compact_absorption`, `actual_hold_source_threshold` line 342, whose η-log-derivative hypothesis is again the bounded jet). (b) p. 155, the §B.7 shape transition: `NavierStokes/MatchingConeBounds.lean` follows the same substitute route — `shapeModel_zero_lower` (line 107) again reduces to χ = 0 (through `chi_zero_imp_H_zero` and `gradient_zero_of_chi_zero`, NaturalEntrance.lean:63 and :70), and `shapeModel_uniform_lower` (line 129) absorbs by compactness, with the η-log-derivative of the profile entering only through a bounded jet \|g\| ≤ B. So the surrounding results — (B.17), the source bound of (B.23), and the transition source bound — are all proved without (B.20) as written. - **(B.21)**, p. 149, proof of Proposition B.3: different. Neither line of (B.21) is stated in the Lean; both are replaced by exact identities, and the error terms the paper estimates are carried symbolically instead. First line: ξ₀ = Λζ_* with ζ_* = -LH_*/(H_*²+σ_*²) is `realGradient` (NaturalAxisCoefficients.lean:51), and `gradient_identity` states H_* · ζ_* = -Lχ exactly, i.e. -H_* ξ₀ = ΛLχ with no error at all; `realAmplitude_logDerivative` identifies (log φ_*)_η with ξ₀ exactly. The paper's O_{σ_*}(√χ) arises only because its H_c is the actual transport coefficient rather than H_*; in the Lean that discrepancy is written out as H_c = Hs + (1/Λ)·d·u and the resulting term appears in `source_remainder_algebra` as the exact summand -d·u·κ pushed into the remainder, never bounded by σ_*^{-1}√χ. The estimate \|H_*\|/(H_*²+σ_*²) ≤ σ_*^{-1}√χ quoted in the paper right after the display is not in the repository: a search for any statement pairing √ with χ returned nothing of this shape. Second line: the Lean has no inequality for -H_c(log φ)_η; the extra piece (log Φ)_η of the full log-derivative is left in the remainder (the φEta/φ slot) and absorbed later by compactness, not by increasing Λ and Young's inequality. The .95 = 19/20 allocation of the paper's last step does surface, but one level up, at the level of S_q: `source_uniform_lower` (and the `EntranceProfile.source_lower` field it populates, NaturalEntrance.lean:1099) keeps 1/20 of ΛLχ to absorb the whole remainder, which is the same bookkeeping as the paper's 'allocating a total of .05ΛLχ'. The route differs; (B.17) is still proved. - **display 154.1**, p. 154, proof of Lemma B.7: lean weaker. The Lean proves this step in increment form and with a larger constant. `logarithmic_error_from_stock` (ReferenceEndpointRate.lean:199-204) states \|∂_η^n(log φ(X_i) − log φ(X_0))\| ≤ (M+1)·R.finalTime, where R.finalTime = log(110/R.radius0) and R.radius0 = 4/Λ. Those are the paper's own values, not a specialization: p.150 reads "Let X0 = 4/Λ, Xb = 100, and Xi = 110" (text.txt:7827), so R.finalTime is exactly the paper's L_0 = log(X_i/X_0) and the two agree here. M bounds the η-jets of R.angularStock (= profileStockOne ∘ chart = X·angularLag/L, the paper's p_{1,r}), and `exists_reference_angularStock_bound` (ReferenceEndpointRate.lean:174-178) produces such an M uniformly in the amplitude choice q : Choice d Λ — the role p.154 gives M_k, "the amplitude-independent bounds for p1,r, ns,r from Lemma B.4" (text.txt:8032), Lemma B.4 being the parameter-norm bound for p_{1,r} on [X_0, X_i] independent of large C and of 0 < t1 ≤ t̄ (text.txt:7842-7843). DIRECTION OF THE COMPARISON. The printed display bounds a norm, ‖log φ(X_i)‖ ≤ ‖log φ_* + log Φ(4,·)‖ + ½(M_k+.8)L_0, and a bound on a norm never yields a bound on a difference, which is what ReferenceEndpointRate.lean:203-204 states; so the display as printed neither implies nor is implied by the Lean statement. The paper's step in the increment form it is derived from is ‖log φ(X_i) − log φ_* − log Φ(4,·)‖_{C^k_η} ≤ (½M_k + .4)L_0, and that implies the Lean's (M+1)L_0 increment bound (with M = M_k, since ½M_k + .4 ≤ M_k + 1 for M_k ≥ 0), not conversely. The Lean constant is therefore strictly larger than the paper's for every M ≥ 0, and the Lean statement is the weaker one. Two slacknesses account for the gap: `controlled_product_bound` (TransitionRamp.lean:604-605) proves \|a·(−(d·z)/2)\| ≤ d·M instead of the sharp d·M/2, so the ½ in front of M_k is dropped; and inside `logarithmic_error_from_stock` the post-interpolation slope term is bounded by \|if n=0 then (2/5)·step else 0\| ≤ 1 (lines 216-220) instead of by 2/5 = .4. The nearest thing in the repository to the paper's κ-damped slope is `logField_positive_jet_error_bound` (TransitionRamp.lean:628-634), which for n ≠ 0 gives the sharper M·(T + κ·y); it is still weaker than ½M_k·L_0, and it is not the lemma `exists_endpointLog_bound` calls, so it does not change the relation. The display's printed shape, with ‖log φ_* + log Φ(4,·)‖ on the right, is reached only after `jet_transfer` against L = log q.C + R.initialLog inside `exists_endpointLog_bound` (ReferenceEndpointRate.lean:253-271); that is why the first declaration is tagged `implies` rather than `states`. One encoding point, not counted as the defect: the Lean replaces the C^k_η norm by the per-order per-point family `∀ n ≤ N, ∀ η ∈ J, \|iteratedDeriv n … η\| ≤ …`. Severity low because Lemma B.7's conclusion (B.32) asserts only ‖ℓ_i‖_{C^k_η} ≤ B_k for some amplitude-independent B_k, and `exists_endpointLog_bound` supplies exactly such a B_k, namely B + (\|log 220 / 2\| + (M+1)\|Y\|); the larger constant enlarges B_k but the surrounding result is still proved. Lean: `NavierStokes.ReferenceEndpointRate.logarithmic_error_from_stock` (implies, NavierStokes/ReferenceEndpointRate.lean:199); `NavierStokes.ReferenceEndpointRate.exists_endpointLog_bound` (contains, NavierStokes/ReferenceEndpointRate.lean:263); `NavierStokes.ReferenceEndpointRate.exists_reference_angularStock_bound` (uses, NavierStokes/ReferenceEndpointRate.lean:174); `NavierStokes.TransitionRamp.controlled_product_bound` (uses, NavierStokes/TransitionRamp.lean:604); `NavierStokes.TransitionRamp.logField_positive_jet_error_bound` (uses, NavierStokes/TransitionRamp.lean:628).

The 1/Λ correction to H_c (shapeRemainder's Hc = H + t·d·v 1, line 85) is again handled by the compactness perturbation. The surrounding result is nevertheless proved: actual_shape_source (MatchingConeBounds.lean:732) gives 1 < 5/4 < sourceQ on the transition, and actual_shape_relaxed (line 776) carries it through to the relaxed cone. - **(B.37)**, p. 156, proof of Proposition B.8: different. The identity P_c = (X_R x/L)G is stated, as `physical_projection`: the physical projection equals c.radius · familyProjection = X_R · (x/L)(Q_s + b N_s/(aE)), and the Lean's second shear component is b = shearB = -2XU_X/E, which is the paper's -b_s (the paper writes the shear vector as s = (a, -b_s)), so Q_s + bN_s/(aE) = Q_s - b_sN_s/(aE) = G. The two inequalities are not stated. The Lean never forms G separately and never uses the constants 6/7 and 3/7: instead `model_margins` bounds the whole projection from below by e^{-8}/2, by perturbation from the ideal control v = 0, where b = 0 and the projection reduces to xQ_s/L ≥ e^{-8} (`zero_projection_lower`, line 690, using Q_s ≥ 1 at the ideal profile); the floor is then multiplied by the radius to give 2 < P_c (line 1245). So the paper's chain G ≥ (6/7)Q_s ≥ 3Q_min/7 is replaced by a direct floor on P_c/X_R, and the conclusion the paper draws from (B.37) — P_c > 2 after increasing X_R, with no tolerance shrinking with X_R — is reached. - **(C.16)**, p. 162, proof of Proposition C.2: lean weaker. The paper bounds all η-derivative orders of the stress-vector difference, Σ_{j=0}^m \|∂_η^j(p_{s,N} - p_s)\| ≤ C_m/N, for every m. What I found in Lean is the order-0 statement only: both profile_stocks_rate declarations bound \|profileStockOne (P n) - profileStockOne Q\| and \|profileStockTwo (P n) - profileStockTwo Q\| pointwise on the compact parameter set S, with no η-derivative of the difference bounded. (Lean's two stocks are the two components of the paper's p_s: profileStockOne = X·angularLag/L and profileStockTwo = X·axialLag/(L·E).) The Lean input is one η-derivative deeper than its output — profileData carries the row values together with their first parameterPartial, and profileHistory_first_jet_bound (ModulatedStockBounds.lean:283) feeds it a FiniteJetBound 1 — which matches the paper's own remark that order m of the stocks consumes order m+1 of the histories, at m = 0 only. Severity low rather than medium because I traced the paper's two uses of (C.16) in the proof of Proposition C.2 — preserving the cone inequality between the interval 𝒥 and the first correction interval, and preserving it inside the correction interval — and both are inequalities between stock values at a point, needing only j = 0. I did not find a higher-order η-jet rate for the stocks anywhere in the modulation files; the nearest higher-order statement, ActivationStocks.log_stocks_uniform_jets (:711), is about the activation ramp parameters (T, κ), not about the N-modulation, so it is not a counterpart. - **display 164.3**, p. 164, proof of Proposition C.3: different. What differs is the UNIFORMITY of kappa over the closed annulus, not the formula: the paper's two inequalities themselves, at kappa = 0, are carried pointwise by the Lean, in the paper's own quantities, over the whole open annulus. `FullTrueCone v` asserts `TrueConeLoop.InTrueCone p_1 p_2 A C` at every p with activeLeft W < p.1 < activeRight W and p.2 ∈ Icc (-1) 1 — the paper's range X_a < X < X_b, -1 ≤ eta ≤ 1. Unfolding `InTrueCone` with A = a, C = c, t_s = C/A, so that v_s = A(1 + (C/A)^2) = a(1 + t_s^2), P_c = p_1 + p_2 t_s and J_c = p_2 - p_1 t_s exactly as in (4.20), it gives 0 < a, 2 < v_s, 2 < P_c and v_s < coneBound P_c J_c; then `true_cone_iff` with hv : 2 < v_s turns the last two into v_s < P_c ∧ (v_s - 2) J_c^2 < 2 (P_c - v_s)^2. Dividing both by \|p_s - (a, -b_s)\|^2 > 0 and substituting the two identities of eq-p164-2 (n_theta + t_s n_z = (P_c - v_s)/\|p_s - s\| and n_z - t_s n_theta = J_c/\|p_s - s\|) gives n_theta + t_s n_z > 0 and (v_s - 2)(n_z - t_s n_theta)^2 < 2 (n_theta + t_s n_z)^2 — the paper's display at kappa = 0, with both inequalities strict. This is a proved statement for the constructed witness: `exists_weighted_profile` produces F, W, d, v with `FullTrueCone v` as its first conjunct. What is not stated anywhere found is a single kappa > 0 that makes both bounds uniform over the whole CLOSED annulus, which is what the paper's display asserts and what its later compactness use needs. The Lean obtains uniformity by two other routes instead. (a) `inner_direction_positive` and `outer_direction_positive` give a uniform margin eps for `directionProjection` and `directionGap`, but only on the two EDGE COLLARS, not on the whole range X_a < X < X_b; and there the margin is normalised against a different quantity. Writing the Lean direction as n = unitOfTilt t = (1, t)/sqrt(1 + t^2), so that n_theta = 1/sqrt(1 + t^2) and n_z = t/sqrt(1 + t^2), one has 1 + s t = (n_theta + s n_z)/n_theta and t - s = (n_z - s n_theta)/n_theta. Hence Lean's `eps ≤ directionProjection s t` reads n_theta + s n_z ≥ eps*n_theta, and Lean's `eps ≤ directionGap v s t` reads 2(n_theta + s n_z)^2 - (v - 2)(n_z - s n_theta)^2 ≥ eps*n_theta^2, whereas the paper's two inequalities are n_theta + t_s n_z ≥ kappa and 2(n_theta + t_s n_z)^2 - (v_s - 2)(n_z - t_s n_theta)^2 ≥ kappa*(n_theta + t_s n_z)^2. So both margins are proportional, but to powers of n_theta rather than to the constant kappa and to the projection respectively; the two are interconvertible only given bounds on n_theta, which these declarations do not carry. (b) On the CLOSED annulus the cone is additionally stated spectrally: `exists_closed_direction` gives a continuous unit direction field satisfying `ReferenceCone ∧ TargetCone` everywhere on Icc (-1) 1 ×ˢ Icc (leftEdge W) (rightEdge W), with `TargetCone` packaging inwardness (⟪T, normalDirection g⟫ < 0) and a strict ratio bound rather than an explicit kappa; `closed_shear_positive` supplies the accompanying a > 0, v_s > 2 on the closed rectangle. No declaration was found that states a single kappa ∈ (0, 2) satisfying both of the paper's inequalities simultaneously over the closed rectangle in the paper's normalisation; the uniform margin re-appears downstream only in the spectral variables. Per calibration rule 2 this is a uniformity/packaging difference, not a weaker mathematical statement of the formula itself: the formula holds pointwise at kappa = 0 by (FullTrueCone, InTrueCone, true_cone_iff) and the surrounding Proposition C.3 cone clause is still proved. - **(7.41)**, p. 87, statement of Corollary 7.8: equivalent. For the constructed fields the Lean states the display in pieces, on the same domain as the paper. Algebraic part. incrementTensor_split (NavierStokes/SignedMeanGain.lean:419, via covariance_increment_split :154) gives C(U+V) - C(U) = B(P,T) + signedRemainder. signedRemainder_exact (NavierStokes/LabelSumBounds.lean:802) rewrites the remainder as B(U-P,T) + B(U,K) + C(T+K), which is B(E, L^as Sigma) + B(U,R) + C(V) under P = W_0^as, E = U - P, T = L^as Sigma, K = R, V = T + K. These need only a SignedFamily and Assembly (angular continuity), no NativeData. This is the identity used on the actual path in CrossBasedMeanComposition.signed_mean_gain_of_cross_defects (NavierStokes/CrossBasedMeanComposition.lean:140, lines 167-183). Sigma substitution B(W_0^as, L^as Sigma) = Sigma. - family_cross_eq (NavierStokes/ActualSignedMeanBinding.lean:644) identifies crossTensor with actualCross. - requested_cross_factor (:442) proves, on every band n and every x in the actual strip domain, meanBar(actualCross 0 i.succ) n x = partitionFactor * requestedStress. - partitionFactor_eq_one (NavierStokes/ActualPrimaryCovariance.lean:508) gives partitionFactor = 1 whenever physicalScale n x <= Q N, N = (choice B N0).prepared.N. It has no band hypothesis. - requested_cross_tail (:463) and family_requested_cross_tail (:655) package this for n >= N+1 via physicalScale_tail (ActualPrimaryCovariance.lean:527). Why this is the paper's domain. p. 64: labels are l >= l0 (6.8), and the active part of each band has 0 < q < q_big <= 2^(-l0). chi_l is supported in q/Q in [1/2,2], so the paper's truncated squared partition sums to one exactly for q < 2^(-l0). The Lean labels likewise have band >= N (BaseChartJets.CellIndex, NavierStokes/BaseChartJets.lean:825), Q n = 2^(-n), physicalScale n x = Q n * coordinateQ (CorrectionInitialization.ActualPrimary.physicalScale, NavierStokes/CorrectionInitialization.lean:5176), and dyadicMask m is supported on (Q m / 2, 2 Q m) (SquaredPartition.dyadicMask_support, NavierStokes/SquaredPartition.lean:458). Under N = l0 and physicalScale = q, the hypothesis physicalScale <= Q N is the paper's q <= 2^(-l0). So (7.41) follows on the paper's domain by combining the declarations above with linearity of meanBar. What the Lean adds. The Lean strip runs over all bands n in N, so physical scale goes up to 2, beyond the paper's q_big. There the partition over labels of band >= N is incomplete, and the Lean states requested_cross_defect (:475): the difference is -missingWeight N (physicalScale n x) * requestedStress. The defect is kept as CrossBasedMeanComposition.crossDefect (:26) and shown to lie in every MeanClass exponent because it vanishes for n >= tailStart = N + 1 (SignedCrossDefectClass.residual_defects_all_exponents_of_primitive, called at CorrectionAnalyticStep.lean:582; cross_tail supplied at ActualCyclePreservation.lean:599). Those points have physicalScale > Q N: for n <= N-1 this holds because coordinateQ > 1/2 on the strip, and on band N it is the part with coordinateQ > 1. They lie outside the paper's domain, so this is extra Lean content, not a missing part of the display. Encoding points, unchanged: - Lean's bilinearCovariance is the theta-average alone, and the Sigma identity is stated after meanBar. - The Lean algebraic identity is for the full 3x3 tensor; the Sigma identity is for entries (0,1), (0,2). - The Lean Sigma is requestedStress / physicalSigma built from the state's theta and axial residuals, for arbitrary context and state, not an arbitrary Sigma in M_alpha. - The Lean algebraic identity needs no class hypotheses. The BandReindexedSignedMeanGain and CorrectionStep native theorems take the same structure. ActualWaveRegularity.NativeData (:47) is unrelated.
